Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, thanks. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’m kind of losing the ability to keep all the moving parts organized. I mean, I’m used to having a lot going on, and in software there are always overlapping priorities, but lately it feels like I can’t sort the pieces in my head anymore.

There’ll be patent notes I’m supposed to be keeping track of, preparation for a workshop, gallery deadlines, team decisions that need follow-up—just all these separate threads—and instead of being able to step back and say, “Okay, this is the important thing, this is next,” I end up grabbing whatever is loudest or most urgent-feeling in the moment. And then later I realize I spent my time on the wrong thing, or I left something important sitting there because it wasn’t making noise right in front of me.

It’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care or don’t understand the work. It’s more like the organizing system in my head just… doesn’t hold together reliably anymore. That’s really what made me think I should talk to someone.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s definitely not completely new. It’s… more visible now, I think, because there are more consequences and more things to juggle, but when I look back, there were signs pretty early.

In grade school, I remember getting comments from teachers that were basically, “He’s bright, but he daydreams,” or “He needs to remember to turn things in.” And that was the maddening thing—I’d sometimes actually finish the homework, but then it wouldn’t make it from my folder to the teacher, or I’d lose the folder, or it would be in my backpack and I just… wouldn’t hand it in. So it wasn’t always that I couldn’t do the work. It was like the chain between doing it and actually completing the whole process would break.

And this was before adolescence, really. Like elementary school stuff. I don’t remember it as one dramatic problem at the time, more like a steady pattern of little failures around organization that adults kept pointing out.

activities.\n  manifestation: I’m constantly hunting for things I need to work, like my USB-C adapters, stylus, sketchbook, or even my badge, and it can derail a whole morning because I have no memory of where I set them down.\n- key: S008\n  name: Distractibility\n  description: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n  manifestation: If Slack pings, a browser tab flashes, or even a visual idea pops into my head, I can lose the thread of what I was coding and spend twenty minutes following some unrelated design rabbit hole.\n- key: S012\n  name: Feeling_Restless\n  description: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: On long design calls I feel this pressure building in my body to get up, so I pace around my office or shift from chair to chair even when I’m trying to look focused.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: After late-night work I’ll try to sit quietly or read something nontechnical, but within a few minutes I’m tapping, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something instead of actually winding down.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Looking back, this was there in grade school too—my teachers wrote that I was bright but daydreamed, forgot to turn in finished assignments, and lost homework folders long before I was a teenager.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: It has started to damage my work reputation; I missed a patent filing checkpoint and a difficult architecture review left collaborators questioning whether I can lead the larger project.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been a real problem for about a year and a month, since the award pushed me into leading more coordination-heavy work.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / software_developer / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S005\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—thanks for coming today. Can you tell me what's been bothering you or what brings you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’m kind of losing the ability to keep all the moving parts organized. I mean, I’m used to having a lot going on, and in software there are always overlapping priorities, but lately it feels like I can’t sort the pieces in my head anymore.\n\nThere’ll be patent notes I’m supposed to be keeping track of, preparation for a workshop, gallery deadlines, team decisions that need follow-up—just all these separate threads—and instead of being able to step back and say, “Okay, this is the important thing, this is next,” I end up grabbing whatever is loudest or most urgent-feeling in the moment. And then later I realize I spent my time on the wrong thing, or I left something important sitting there because it wasn’t making noise right in front of me.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care or don’t understand the work. It’s more like the organizing system in my head just… doesn’t hold together reliably anymore. That’s really what made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of difficulty organizing and prioritizing tasks been a pattern for you since childhood or adolescence, or is this a new change that's developed recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not completely new. It’s… more visible now, I think, because there are more consequences and more things to juggle, but when I look back, there were signs pretty early.\n\nIn grade school, I remember getting comments from teachers that were basically, “He’s bright, but he daydreams,” or “He needs to remember to turn things in.” And that was the maddening thing—I’d sometimes actually finish the homework, but then it wouldn’t make it from my folder to the teacher, or I’d lose the folder, or it would be in my backpack and I just… wouldn’t hand it in. So it wasn’t always that I couldn’t do the work. It was like the chain between doing it and actually completing the whole process would break.\n\nAnd this was before adolescence, really. Like elementary school stuff. I don’t remember it as one dramatic problem at the time, more like a steady pattern of little failures around organization that adults kept pointing out.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble organizing tasks, have you been experiencing excessive worry or anxiety about multiple areas of your life that's hard to control, on most days over the past several months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things when I realize I’ve dropped something or I’m behind, and then I can kind of spiral for a bit about the consequences or who I’ve let down. But it feels more tied to specific situations, if that makes sense—not like a constant, across-the-board worry about everything most days.\n\nI don’t think I’m walking around for months unable to control worry about multiple areas of life. It’s more that when something gets missed or feels chaotic, then I get stressed about it. But the baseline problem feels less like “I’m worrying all the time” and more like I can’t reliably keep track of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel restless or fidgety, have trouble sitting still, or find yourself interrupting others or blurting things out, even now as an adult?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, some of that, definitely more on the restless side than the blurting-out side, I think.

Like on long design calls, especially the ones where we’re talking through architecture decisions or reviewing something abstract for an hour or more, I can feel this sort of pressure building in my body. It’s not exactly panic or anything, it’s more like I suddenly can’t stay contained in the chair. I’ll start shifting around, changing position, leaning forward, leaning back, and then eventually I’ll just get up and pace around my office while I’m still on the call.

I try to make it look normal, like I’m just thinking or I turned my camera off for some practical reason, but really I’m doing it because sitting still starts to feel almost physically irritating. If I have to stay on camera, I’ll move from one chair to another or keep adjusting how I’m sitting and try to look like I’m focused, even though part of my attention is going into managing that restless feeling.

But it feels more tied to specific situations, if that makes sense—not like a constant, across-the-board worry about everything most days.\n\nI don’t think I’m walking around for months unable to control worry about multiple areas of life. It’s more that when something gets missed or feels chaotic, then I get stressed about it. But the baseline problem feels less like “I’m worrying all the time” and more like I can’t reliably keep track of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, have trouble sitting still, or find yourself interrupting others or blurting things out, even now as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some of that, definitely more on the restless side than the blurting-out side, I think.\n\nLike on long design calls, especially the ones where we’re talking through architecture decisions or reviewing something abstract for an hour or more, I can feel this sort of pressure building in my body. It’s not exactly panic or anything, it’s more like I suddenly can’t stay contained in the chair. I’ll start shifting around, changing position, leaning forward, leaning back, and then eventually I’ll just get up and pace around my office while I’m still on the call.\n\nI try to make it look normal, like I’m just thinking or I turned my camera off for some practical reason, but really I’m doing it because sitting still starts to feel almost physically irritating. If I have to stay on camera, I’ll move from one chair to another or keep adjusting how I’m sitting and try to look like I’m focused, even though part of my attention is going into managing that restless feeling.\n\n[Last Question]\nGrowing up, did these attention and restlessness difficulties cause noticeable problems in more than one setting at the time—for example, both at school and at home?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Work is the clearest place it’s showing up, but it doesn’t feel contained to just work anymore.

The thing that’s been scaring me lately is that it’s starting to affect how people see me professionally. I’m not just having private chaos where I’m stressed but somehow still holding it together. I missed a patent filing checkpoint, which… that was not a small “oops, forgot to reply to an email” kind of thing. There were steps I was supposed to keep track of, and I didn’t keep the process moving the way I should have. And then there was this architecture review that was already difficult and high-stakes, and I wasn’t organized enough going into it or during it. The discussion got messy, and afterward I got the sense that some collaborators were really questioning whether I could lead the larger project.

That hit me pretty hard, because I’ve always thought of myself as technically capable. Like, if you put me in front of a hard problem, I can usually reason through it. But leading something bigger requires tracking threads, following up, knowing what’s been decided, knowing what still needs to happen, and I’m not doing that reliably enough. So yes, it’s causing real problems. It’s not just annoying or inefficient anymore; it’s damaging my reputation and making people doubt me in roles where I’m supposed to be dependable.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. One example is evenings at home, especially after I’ve been working late.

I’ll tell myself I’m going to do something quiet to come down from the day, like sit on the couch and read a normal book or something nontechnical—something that isn’t code, isn’t architecture, isn’t another screen full of problems. In my head it sounds simple: just sit there, read for twenty minutes, let my brain settle.

But what actually happens is that within a few minutes I’m not really reading anymore. I’ll realize my foot is tapping, or I’m shifting around, or I’ve reread the same paragraph three times and I’m not taking it in. Then I’ll check my phone “just for a second,” or I’ll suddenly get up because some small thing in the room feels like it needs adjusting—the blinds, the thermostat, a dish in the sink, something on the table. It’s not that any of those things are urgent. It’s more like I can’t tolerate just sitting quietly long enough to wind down.

And then the problem is I don’t actually recover. I meant to have a calm half hour, but instead I spend it bouncing between the book, my phone, and little pointless tasks around the house. By the time I go to bed, I don’t feel like I had any real quiet time. It’s frustrating because it looks like nothing from the outside—I’m just at home, sitting there—but internally it feels like I can’t stay with the thing I chose to do.

activities.\n  manifestation: I’m constantly hunting for things I need to work, like my USB-C adapters, stylus, sketchbook, or even my badge, and it can derail a whole morning because I have no memory of where I set them down.\n- key: S008\n  name: Distractibility\n  description: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n  manifestation: If Slack pings, a browser tab flashes, or even a visual idea pops into my head, I can lose the thread of what I was coding and spend twenty minutes following some unrelated design rabbit hole.\n- key: S012\n  name: Feeling_Restless\n  description: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: On long design calls I feel this pressure building in my body to get up, so I pace around my office or shift from chair to chair even when I’m trying to look focused.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: After late-night work I’ll try to sit quietly or read something nontechnical, but within a few minutes I’m tapping, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something instead of actually winding down.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Looking back, this was there in grade school too—my teachers wrote that I was bright but daydreamed, forgot to turn in finished assignments, and lost homework folders long before I was a teenager.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: It has started to damage my work reputation; I missed a patent filing checkpoint and a difficult architecture review left collaborators questioning whether I can lead the larger project.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been a real problem for about a year and a month, since the award pushed me into leading more coordination-heavy work.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / software_developer / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S005\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Growing up, did these attention and restlessness difficulties cause noticeable problems in more than one setting at the time—for example, both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not completely sure. School is easier for me to remember because there were external markers, like teachers commenting or assignments not getting handled properly. Home is fuzzier, because I don’t know how much of what happened there was seen as “a problem” versus just me being a kid, or my parents compensating for me without anyone labeling it that way.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, clearly it was in every setting,” because I don’t have that kind of clean memory. I can say the school side was noticeable. At home, I have impressions, but not the same specific evidence in my head. It’s more like… I suspect there were things, but I’d have to think harder or maybe ask family to know how obvious it was at the time.\n\nDoctor: Right now, do these organizational and attention difficulties cause real problems for you in more than one area of your life—for example, both at work and in your personal or home life?\n\nPatient: Yes. Work is the clearest place it’s showing up, but it doesn’t feel contained to just work anymore.\n\nThe thing that’s been scaring me lately is that it’s starting to affect how people see me professionally. I’m not just having private chaos where I’m stressed but somehow still holding it together. I missed a patent filing checkpoint, which… that was not a small “oops, forgot to reply to an email” kind of thing. There were steps I was supposed to keep track of, and I didn’t keep the process moving the way I should have. The discussion got messy, and afterward I got the sense that some collaborators were really questioning whether I could lead the larger project.\n\nThat hit me pretty hard, because I’ve always thought of myself as technically capable. Like, if you put me in front of a hard problem, I can usually reason through it. It’s not just annoying or inefficient anymore; it’s damaging my reputation and making people doubt me in roles where I’m supposed to be dependable.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of how this disorganization or restlessness has caused a problem in your personal life or at home—something outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’ll realize my foot is tapping, or I’m shifting around, or I’ve reread the same paragraph three times and I’m not taking it in. By the time I go to bed, I don’t feel like I had any real quiet time. I don’t think so. At least, nothing that stands out to me as a distinct period of several days where I was unusually up or euphoric or operating on much less sleep and still feeling fine.

So it wasn’t always that I couldn’t do the work. It was like the chain between doing it and actually completing the whole process would break.\n\nAnd this was before adolescence, really. Like elementary school stuff. I don’t remember it as one dramatic problem at the time, more like a steady pattern of little failures around organization that adults kept pointing out.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble organizing tasks, have you been experiencing excessive worry or anxiety about multiple areas of your life that's hard to control, on most days over the past several months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things when I realize I’ve dropped something or I’m behind, and then I can kind of spiral for a bit about the consequences or who I’ve let down. But it feels more tied to specific situations, if that makes sense—not like a constant, across-the-board worry about everything most days.\n\nI don’t think I’m walking around for months unable to control worry about multiple areas of life. It’s more that when something gets missed or feels chaotic, then I get stressed about it. But the baseline problem feels less like “I’m worrying all the time” and more like I can’t reliably keep track of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, have trouble sitting still, or find yourself interrupting others or blurting things out, even now as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some of that, definitely more on the restless side than the blurting-out side, I think.\n\nLike on long design calls, especially the ones where we’re talking through architecture decisions or reviewing something abstract for an hour or more, I can feel this sort of pressure building in my body. It’s not exactly panic or anything, it’s more like I suddenly can’t stay contained in the chair. I’ll start shifting around, changing position, leaning forward, leaning back, and then eventually I’ll just get up and pace around my office while I’m still on the call.\n\nI try to make it look normal, like I’m just thinking or I turned my camera off for some practical reason, but really I’m doing it because sitting still starts to feel almost physically irritating. If I have to stay on camera, I’ll move from one chair to another or keep adjusting how I’m sitting and try to look like I’m focused, even though part of my attention is going into managing that restless feeling.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, did these attention and restlessness difficulties cause noticeable problems in more than one setting at the time—for example, both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not completely sure. School is easier for me to remember because there were external markers, like teachers commenting or assignments not getting handled properly. Home is fuzzier, because I don’t know how much of what happened there was seen as “a problem” versus just me being a kid, or my parents compensating for me without anyone labeling it that way.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, clearly it was in every setting,” because I don’t have that kind of clean memory. I can say the school side was noticeable. At home, I have impressions, but not the same specific evidence in my head. It’s more like… I suspect there were things, but I’d have to think harder or maybe ask family to know how obvious it was at the time.\n\nDoctor: Right now, do these organizational and attention difficulties cause real problems for you in more than one area of your life—for example, both at work and in your personal or home life?\n\nPatient: Yes. Work is the clearest place it’s showing up, but it doesn’t feel contained to just work anymore.\n\nThe thing that’s been scaring me lately is that it’s starting to affect how people see me professionally. I’m not just having private chaos where I’m stressed but somehow still holding it together. I missed a patent filing checkpoint, which… that was not a small “oops, forgot to reply to an email” kind of thing. There were steps I was supposed to keep track of, and I didn’t keep the process moving the way I should have. And then there was this architecture review that was already difficult and high-stakes, and I wasn’t organized enough going into it or during it. The discussion got messy, and afterward I got the sense that some collaborators were really questioning whether I could lead the larger project.\n\nThat hit me pretty hard, because I’ve always thought of myself as technically capable. Like, if you put me in front of a hard problem, I can usually reason through it. But leading something bigger requires tracking threads, following up, knowing what’s been decided, knowing what still needs to happen, and I’m not doing that reliably enough. So yes, it’s causing real problems. It’s not just annoying or inefficient anymore; it’s damaging my reputation and making people doubt me in roles where I’m supposed to be dependable.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of how this disorganization or restlessness has caused a problem in your personal life or at home—something outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. One example is evenings at home, especially after I’ve been working late.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m going to do something quiet to come down from the day, like sit on the couch and read a normal book or something nontechnical—something that isn’t code, isn’t architecture, isn’t another screen full of problems. In my head it sounds simple: just sit there, read for twenty minutes, let my brain settle.\n\nBut what actually happens is that within a few minutes I’m not really reading anymore. I’ll realize my foot is tapping, or I’m shifting around, or I’ve reread the same paragraph three times and I’m not taking it in. Then I’ll check my phone “just for a second,” or I’ll suddenly get up because some small thing in the room feels like it needs adjusting—the blinds, the thermostat, a dish in the sink, something on the table. It’s not that any of those things are urgent. It’s more like I can’t tolerate just sitting quietly long enough to wind down.\n\nAnd then the problem is I don’t actually recover. I meant to have a calm half hour, but instead I spend it bouncing between the book, my phone, and little pointless tasks around the house. By the time I go to bed, I don’t feel like I had any real quiet time. It’s frustrating because it looks like nothing from the outside—I’m just at home, sitting there—but internally it feels like I can’t stay with the thing I chose to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had distinct periods lasting several days or more where you felt unusually energetic, elated, or irritable, needed much less sleep than usual, or noticed your thoughts racing more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. At least, nothing that stands out to me as a distinct period of several days where I was unusually up or euphoric or operating on much less sleep and still feeling fine.\n\nI’ve definitely had times where my mind feels busy, or I’m keyed up because there’s a lot going on, but that feels more tied to stress or deadlines, not like a separate “episode,” if that makes sense. And if I sleep less, I usually feel it—I don’t become this super-productive, energized version of myself. I’m more tired and scattered.\n\nI’m less sure about irritability, because I can get irritable when I’m overwhelmed, but I wouldn’t describe it as days of being unusually irritable with extra energy or racing thoughts beyond my normal. So my answer would be no, not that I’ve noticed."
  }
]

Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, it’s definitely not completely new. It’s… more visible now, I think, because there are more consequences and more things to juggle, but when I look back, there were signs pretty early.\n\nIn grade school, I remember getting comments from teachers that were basically, “He’s bright, but he daydreams,” or “He needs to remember to turn things in.” And that was the maddening thing—I’d sometimes actually finish the homework, but then it wouldn’t make it from my folder to the teacher, or I’d lose the folder, or it would be in my backpack and I just… wouldn’t hand it in. So it wasn’t always that I couldn’t do the work. It was like the chain between doing it and actually completing the whole process would break.\n\nAnd this was before adolescence, really. Like elementary school stuff. I don’t remember it as one dramatic problem at the time, more like a steady pattern of little failures around organization that adults kept pointing out.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression. Home is fuzzier, because I don’t know how much of what happened there was seen as “a problem” versus just me being a kid, or my parents compensating for me without anyone labeling it that way.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, clearly it was in every setting,” because I don’t have that kind of clean memory. It’s more like… I suspect there were things, but I’d have to think harder or maybe ask family to know how obvious it was at the time.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========
{"confirmed":["S004"],"denied":[],"reasoning":{"S004":"The patient reports that at school there were noticeable issues such as assignments not getting handled properly, implying difficulty following through on or completing schoolwork."}}
